  • Maturity labels are plain-words labels from the universal research maturity scale (E1 Established, E2 Reproduced, E3 Engineering-ready, E4 Experimental, E5 Prototype, E6 Integrated, E7 Operational; T1 Theoretical, T2 Hypothetical, T3 Speculative; U Unknown). A label says how mature a technology is; it never turns a guess into a fact.

  • Public records are documented evidence, never established fact. Signature records are design-study, never presented as authentic public record.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. Machine consciousness is theories and testable models only, never an established fact; no claim is made that a machine is conscious, sentient, or aware.
  • Climate restoration records are research only; no proven-intervention claims are made.
  • Post-quantum records are defensive security only.
  • Synthetic biology is design-study only, with biosafety screening; no pathogen, toxin, gene-drive, or synthesis-enabling content appears here.

Terms in Plain Words

database: a database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to. record: a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. slug: a slug is the short web-address name of a database, written with lower-case words joined by hyphens. manifest: the manifest is the master list of all databases in the network, with each database's slug, record kind, identifier prefix, and record count. record kind: a record kind is the label that says what sort of entry a record is, for example a future artificial life record. identifier prefix: an identifier prefix is the fixed letters at the start of every record identifier in one database, for example JAH-ALIFE-. generator: a generator is a program that creates new records or files on demand from a starting number called a seed, instead of storing every output in advance. seed: a seed is a starting number fed to a generator so that the same seed always produces the same output. generator gate: a generator gate is a check a generator must pass before its output is accepted, for example the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind. JCC code: a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, is a short code that points to one exact record, written as JAHDB followed by the database slug, a hash sign, and the record identifier. API: an API, which stands for Application Programming Interface, is a defined way for one program to ask another program for data or work. MCP: an MCP, which stands for Model Context Protocol, is a plug that lets an AI (artificial intelligence) program read a database directly. AI: AI stands for artificial intelligence, which is a program that performs tasks that usually need human thought, such as answering, classifying, or generating. SQLite: SQLite is a small database engine that stores a whole database in one file on disk. SQL: SQL, which stands for Structured Query Language, is the language used to ask questions of a relational database. JSON: JSON, which stands for JavaScript Object Notation, is a plain-text format for storing structured data with named fields. HTML: HTML, which stands for HyperText Markup Language, is the language that marks up the structure of a web page. CSS: CSS, which stands for Cascading Style Sheets, is the language that describes how a web page looks. URL: a URL, which stands for Uniform Resource Locator, is the full web address of a page or file. HTTP: HTTP, which stands for HyperText Transfer Protocol, is the set of rules a browser uses to fetch a page from a web server. HTTPS: HTTPS, which stands for HyperText Transfer Protocol Secure, is HTTP sent over a protected connection. test suite: a test suite is a collection of automated tests that check whether a program behaves as specified. demo: a demo, short for demonstration, is a small working program that runs and shows its result. website interface: a website interface is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses a system. JAH: JAH is the initials of Justin Addam Higgins, the maker name used across the network. design-study: a design-study is a Signature-made design, an invented proposal that is labeled as a design and never presented as an established fact. UNREACHABLE: UNREACHABLE is the label recorded for a resource whose fetch failed, together with the exact reason the fetch returned. maturity label: a maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, from E1 Established through E7 Operational for working technology, and T1 Theoretical through T3 Speculative for ideas not yet tested, and U for Unknown. research question: a research question is the single question a research project sets out to answer. prototype: a prototype is an early working model built to test whether an idea works. simulation: a simulation is a computer model that imitates a real system so it can be studied safely and cheaply. artificial life: artificial life is the study of life-like behaviour in systems built by people, in software, in hardware, or in laboratory chemistry, rather than life that evolved in nature. digital organism: a digital organism is a self-replicating program that lives, competes, and evolves inside a computer, like a living thing in a digital world. cellular automaton: a cellular automaton is a grid of cells that change by simple local rules, step by step, and can produce complex patterns from those rules. programmable material: a programmable material is a material whose properties, such as shape, stiffness, or colour, can be changed on command by a signal such as heat, light, electricity, or a magnetic field. fusion: fusion is the joining of light atomic nuclei to form a heavier nucleus, releasing energy, the process that powers the Sun. plasma: plasma is a hot gas of charged particles, atoms stripped of some of their electrons, that can be held by magnetic fields. tokamak: a tokamak is a doughnut-shaped chamber that holds hot plasma in place with magnetic fields so fusion can happen inside it. stellarator: a stellarator is a twisted chamber that holds hot plasma in place with magnetic fields, like a tokamak but with a more complex twisted shape. tritium: tritium is a heavy form of hydrogen, with one proton and two neutrons, used as a fusion fuel. deuterium: deuterium is a heavy form of hydrogen, with one proton and one neutron, used as a fusion fuel. Q factor: the Q factor, which is the fusion energy gain factor, is the fusion power produced divided by the heating power put in, so Q = 10 means ten times more fusion power out than heating power in. ITER: ITER, which stands for International Thermonuclear Experimental Reactor, is the large international fusion experiment being built in France. JET: JET, which stands for Joint European Torus, is the European fusion experiment in England. NIF: NIF, which stands for National Ignition Facility, is the United States laser fusion experiment in California. DOE: DOE, which stands for Department of Energy, is the United States government department for energy and science. D-T: D-T stands for deuterium-tritium, the fusion fuel pair made of the two heavy forms of hydrogen. DNA: DNA, which stands for deoxyribonucleic acid, is the molecule that carries genetic instructions in living cells. megawatt: a megawatt, shortened to MW, is one million watts, a unit of power. biosafety: biosafety is the set of practices that keep biological work safe, including screening, authorization, and human review.

What Artificial Life Is

JAH-ALIFE-000001 · artificial life identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is What Artificial Life Is. Artificial life is the study of life-like behaviour in systems built by people, in software, in hardware, or in laboratory chemistry, rather than life that evolved in nature. A life-like system is one that shows some of the patterns of living things: it keeps itself going, it makes copies of itself, it changes over time, and it adapts to its surroundings. Artificial life research asks which of those patterns can be built, which need a living body, and which appear on their own when simple rules run for a long time. The field has three branches that this record keeps apart. Soft artificial life builds life-like behaviour in software, such as digital organisms that compete inside a computer. Hard artificial life builds it in hardware, such as robots that move, sense, and adapt. Wet artificial life builds it in laboratory chemistry, such as protocells, which are simple laboratory models of cells. None of these branches claims to have created life itself: they study life-like behaviour, and they say plainly where the model ends and real life begins. This record specifies the identity of the field, its three branches, its research questions, and the line between a model and a living thing. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. The public edition records the research specification as documented public knowledge and established research, with sources that were fetched and checked on 2026-11-17. The Signature edition holds Signature-made designs, each labeled design-study, which is a Signature-made design labeled as a design and never presented as an established fact. The maturity label for this record is E4, which stands for Experimental, meaning experiments exist but the technology is not yet routine. A maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, and E4 marks this work as research, never as a finished or proven system. Synthetic biology content here is design-study only with biosafety screening; no pathogen, no toxin, no gene-drive construct, and no synthesis-enabling content is built.

Digital Organisms

JAH-ALIFE-000002 · digital organisms · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Digital Organisms. A digital organism is a self-replicating program that lives, competes, and evolves inside a computer, like a living thing in a digital world. The organism is a string of instructions that copies itself into computer memory, and copies sometimes carry small changes, which are called mutations, meaning small random changes in the instructions. Organisms compete for the two resources of their world: computer time, which is the processing power they need to run, and memory, which is the storage space they need to live in. Those that copy faster, or that learn to perform rewarded tasks, leave more descendants, and over many generations the population changes, which is evolution in the digital world. Digital organisms are the core object of soft artificial life: they let researchers watch evolution happen in hours instead of millions of years, repeat the experiment exactly, and record every ancestor. This record specifies digital organisms: their anatomy as instruction strings, their world as time and memory, their reproduction as copying with mutation, and their evolution as selection over generations. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Tierra and Early Digital Evolution

JAH-ALIFE-000003 · tierra digital evolution · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Tierra and Early Digital Evolution. Tierra is the early digital evolution system in which programs competed for computer time and memory, built by Thomas Ray and first described in the early 1990s. In Tierra, small programs lived in a shared block of memory, copied themselves, and sometimes made copying mistakes that created new variants. Shorter programs that copied faster spread, and over time the population produced parasites, which are programs that borrow the copying code of others instead of carrying their own, and then hyper-parasites that exploit the parasites. Tierra showed, in a running system, that competition, parasitism, and increasing complexity can appear from simple rules without anyone designing them in advance. The system is a landmark because it was one of the first to show open-ended change in a computer: the population kept producing new forms instead of settling on one winner. This record specifies Tierra: its world, its instruction set, its competition for time and memory, and the ecological roles that appeared, and it records the results as documented research, not as a claim that digital life equals biological life. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Avida and Digital Evolution Research

JAH-ALIFE-000004 · avida digital evolution · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Avida and Digital Evolution Research. Avida is the digital evolution research platform in which programs evolve to perform logic tasks, developed from Tierra's ideas by researchers at Michigan State University and the California Institute of Technology. In Avida, digital organisms live in separate cells, copy themselves with mutations, and can earn extra computer time by performing logic operations such as AND, OR, and NOT, which are the basic yes-no operations of computing. Researchers set the rewards, run the population for thousands of generations, and then read the full ancestry of every organism, because every birth is recorded. Avida has been used to study how complex traits evolve step by step, how cooperation appears, and how populations respond when the environment changes. Its strength is repeatability: the same starting conditions and the same random seed produce the same run, so a result can be checked by running it again. This record specifies Avida: its cell world, its reward system, its ancestry records, and the kinds of experiments it supports, all as documented research. Conway's Game of Life

JAH-ALIFE-000005 · game of life · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Conway's Game of Life. Conway's Game of Life is the cellular automaton in which simple rules produce complex patterns, invented by the mathematician John Horton Conway in 1970. The game runs on a grid of cells, each cell either alive or dead, and each step applies three rules: a live cell with two or three live neighbours stays alive, a live cell with any other number of neighbours dies, and a dead cell with exactly three live neighbours becomes alive. From those three rules come gliders that crawl across the grid, guns that fire gliders forever, and patterns that build copies of themselves. The Game of Life matters to artificial life because it shows emergence, which is complex behaviour arising from simple local rules with no central control, and because it is simple enough for anyone to run and study. It is also a proven universal computer in principle: patterns exist that can compute anything a computer can compute. This record specifies the Game of Life: its grid, its three rules, its famous patterns, and its role as the simplest demonstration of emergence. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Cellular Automata

JAH-ALIFE-000006 · cellular automata · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Cellular Automata. A cellular automaton is a grid of cells that change by simple local rules, step by step, and can produce complex patterns from those rules. Each cell holds a state, such as alive or dead, or a colour, and at each step every cell looks at its neighbours and updates its own state by a fixed rule. The rules are local, meaning each cell only sees its neighbours, and uniform, meaning the same rule applies everywhere, yet the whole grid can produce waves, stable structures, moving patterns, and even self-reproducing forms. Cellular automata are the mathematical ground of much artificial life work: they model growth, infection, traffic, fire, and crystal formation, and they give researchers a laboratory where every state is visible and every run is repeatable. This record specifies cellular automata: grids, states, neighbourhoods, rules, and the four broad kinds of behaviour they show, from settling down to endless change, and it keeps the mathematics separate from any claim about living things. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Self-Replication in Software

JAH-ALIFE-000007 · self-replication in software · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Self-Replication in Software. Self-replication in software is the ability of a program to make a copy of itself, and it is the foundation of digital artificial life, because without copying there is no population and no evolution. A self-replicating program reads its own instructions, writes them into a new place in memory, and starts the copy running, so one program becomes two, and two become four. Copying is never perfect: small mistakes, called mutations, creep in, and those mistakes are the raw material of evolution, because a copy that differs may copy faster, slower, or in a new way. Researchers study self-replication to learn the smallest program that can copy itself, the ways copying can fail, and the ways a population of copiers can be kept stable. The classic demonstration is the quine, a program that prints its own text, and the working form is the digital organism that copies itself into memory. This record specifies self-replication in software: the copy loop, the sources of copying error, the minimum size of a copier, and the checks that keep a runaway population under control. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Artificial Chemistries

JAH-ALIFE-000008 · artificial chemistries · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Chemistries. An artificial chemistry is a simulated chemistry in which molecules react by rules and new forms appear, built to study how chemistry can organize itself without a living cell to guide it. In an artificial chemistry, the molecules are strings of symbols, the reactions are rules that rewrite those strings, and the container is a shared space where molecules meet at random. From those parts come cycles that keep themselves going, sets of molecules that make each other, and long chains that behave like the first steps of metabolism, which is the set of chemical reactions that keep a living thing running. Artificial chemistries matter because they test ideas about the origin of life in a system where every reaction is visible and repeatable, and because they show how order can appear from random meetings without a designer. This record specifies artificial chemistries: molecules as symbol strings, reactions as rewrite rules, containers as meeting places, and the self-keeping cycles that researchers look for, all as simulation research. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Protocells and Minimal Cells

JAH-ALIFE-000009 · protocells and minimal cells · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Protocells and Minimal Cells. A protocell is a simple laboratory model of a cell, built to study how cells may have begun, and a minimal cell is a living cell pared down to the smallest set of parts it needs to live and reproduce. A protocell is usually a bubble of fat-like molecules holding some chemistry inside, and researchers watch whether it can grow, divide, and keep its chemistry going without the full machinery of a modern cell. A minimal cell is built the other way: researchers take a real bacterium and remove genes, which are the instructions in DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid), until the cell barely survives, to learn which genes are truly essential. Both are research models, and both stay inside the boundary of this pair: they are studied as laboratory models with biosafety screening, authorization, and human review, and no pathogen, no toxin, no gene-drive construct, and no synthesis-enabling instruction is built here. This record specifies protocells and minimal cells as documented research: their parts, their tests, and the line between a model and a living organism. Xenobots and Living Robots

JAH-ALIFE-000010 · xenobots and living robots · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Xenobots and Living Robots. A xenobot is a living robot built from frog cells, a published research result in which skin cells and heart muscle cells from a frog embryo were shaped by computer design into a small body that moves, pushes objects, and, in later work, gathers loose cells into new copies of itself. The cells are real living cells, the shape is designed by an evolutionary algorithm, and the result is a machine made of living material rather than metal or plastic. Xenobots matter to artificial life because they show a third path between software and hardware: living material shaped into a designed form, doing designed work, without any genetic engineering of the cells themselves. The research is documented, peer-reviewed, and small in scale, and this record reports it as it stands: a laboratory demonstration, maturity E4 (Experimental), not a product, not a creature with a mind, and making no claim about consciousness. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system. Minimal Genomes

JAH-ALIFE-000011 · minimal genomes · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Minimal Genomes. A minimal genome is the smallest set of genes an organism needs to live and reproduce, and finding it is one of the sharpest questions in biology, because it asks which instructions are truly essential and which are extras. A genome is the full set of genetic instructions in DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid), and a gene is one instruction in that set. Researchers approach the minimal genome two ways: by deleting genes from a real bacterium until it barely survives, and by building a genome from known parts and testing whether a cell can run on it. The best-known result is a laboratory bacterium running on a genome of about 473 genes, a documented research result that shows how much of a natural genome is not strictly needed for life in a rich laboratory medium. This record specifies minimal genomes as documented research: the deletion method, the building method, the test of survival and reproduction, and the limits of what the result means, with biosafety screening and human review stated, and no pathogen, toxin, gene-drive, or synthesis-enabling content built here. Open-Ended Evolution

JAH-ALIFE-000012 · open-ended evolution · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Open-Ended Evolution. Open-ended evolution is evolution that keeps producing new forms without a set end, and it is the property artificial life researchers most want to reproduce, because natural evolution has run for billions of years without stopping, while most computer evolutions settle on one winner and stop changing. An open-ended system needs three things: new variation that keeps appearing, new niches, which are new ways of making a living, that keep opening, and selection that keeps changing what counts as success. Tierra showed early signs of it when parasites and hyper-parasites appeared, and later systems chase it with changing environments, coevolution, which is two populations evolving against each other, and rewards that move as the population improves. No computer system yet matches the open-endedness of nature, and this record says so plainly: the goal is stated, the partial results are documented, and the gap is measured, not hidden. This record specifies open-ended evolution: its three needs, its measures, and the experiments that test for it. Evolutionary Algorithms

JAH-ALIFE-000013 · evolutionary algorithms · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Evolutionary Algorithms. An evolutionary algorithm is a search method that copies the logic of natural selection to solve problems, and it is the workhorse of artificial life research. The method runs in a loop: make a population of candidate solutions, score each one against the problem, keep the best, copy them with small random changes, and repeat. Over many generations the population climbs toward good solutions, including solutions no human designer would have thought of, such as oddly shaped antennas and efficient walking gaits for robots. Evolutionary algorithms are used to design xenobot shapes, to tune cellular automaton rules, and to search for control programs that a human would take years to write by hand. Their strength is that they need no model of the problem, only a way to score a candidate; their weakness is that they can take many generations and can settle on a merely good answer. This record specifies evolutionary algorithms: population, scoring, selection, copying with mutation, and the stopping rules that say when the search is done. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Artificial Ecosystems

JAH-ALIFE-000014 · artificial ecosystems · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Ecosystems. An artificial ecosystem is a simulated world in which many life-like parts interact: producers that make energy, consumers that eat, predators that hunt, and a physical world of space, food, and weather that changes over time. The ecosystem is built from simple rules for each part, and the interest is in what the whole does: food webs that form on their own, populations that rise and fall in cycles, species that appear and vanish, and collapses that follow when one link is removed. Researchers use artificial ecosystems to test ideas about real ecosystems without harming a real one, to study how diversity protects a system, and to learn what makes a system stable or fragile. The models are honest about their limits: they simplify real ecology heavily, and a result in the model is a hypothesis for the field, never a fact about a real forest or reef. This record specifies artificial ecosystems: their parts, their rules, their food webs, and the stability tests researchers run on them. Swarm Behaviour and Artificial Life

JAH-ALIFE-000015 · swarm behaviour · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Swarm Behaviour and Artificial Life. Swarm behaviour is the collective behaviour of many simple agents acting together, and it is one of the clearest examples of emergence in artificial life: no single ant knows the plan of the colony, yet the colony finds food, builds nests, and defends itself. In software, a swarm is a population of agents that follow three local rules: stay close to neighbours, match their speed, and avoid collisions, and from those rules come flocks, schools, and traffic flows that look planned but are not. Researchers use swarms to steer robot teams, to search large spaces, and to model how groups decide without a leader. The rules are simple, the behaviour is robust when single agents fail, and the failure mode is honest: a swarm with the wrong rules can jam, scatter, or chase itself in circles. This record specifies swarm behaviour: the agents, the three local rules, the collective patterns that appear, and the tests that show when a swarm works and when it fails. Artificial Life in Robotics

JAH-ALIFE-000016 · artificial life in robotics · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life in Robotics. Artificial life in robotics is the building of robots that behave in life-like ways: they move, sense, adapt, learn from experience, and keep going when parts of them fail. The work splits into two paths. One path evolves robot controllers in simulation, using evolutionary algorithms to search for control programs, and then moves the best controllers into real robots. The other path builds robots from soft, flexible materials and living cells, as in the xenobot work, so the body itself is life-like. Both paths face the same hard problem, called the reality gap: a controller that works perfectly in simulation may fail in the real world, where friction, noise, and wear are never exactly as modelled. Researchers close the gap by adding noise to the simulation, by testing on real robots early, and by letting the robot keep adapting after it is built. This record specifies artificial life in robotics: the two paths, the reality gap, and the methods that close it, all as documented research. Artificial Life and Complex Systems

JAH-ALIFE-000017 · artificial life and complex systems · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life and Complex Systems. A complex system is a system whose whole behaviour cannot be read off from its parts alone, and artificial life is one of the main ways researchers study such systems, because a digital organism, a swarm, or a cellular automaton is a complex system that can be watched in full. The study looks for shared patterns across very different systems: tipping points where a small change flips the whole system, cascades where one failure triggers many, and self-organization, where order appears without a plan. The tools are models that run many times with different starting points, measures of how ordered or disordered a system is, and maps of which states lead to which futures. The honest limit is that a model is not the world: a cellular automaton that models traffic is not traffic, and its predictions are hypotheses to test, not facts. This record specifies artificial life as complex-systems research: its shared patterns, its tools, and the line between a model result and a world fact. Measuring Complexity in Artificial Life

JAH-ALIFE-000018 · measuring complexity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Measuring Complexity in Artificial Life. Measuring complexity is the problem of saying, in numbers, how complex a life-like system is, so that one run can be compared with another and progress can be checked instead of guessed. Researchers use several measures, and this record names the main ones. One measure counts the information in a system, using the length of the shortest description that captures it. Another measures how much the future depends on the past, which is high in systems with memory. A third counts the distinct behaviours a system shows, such as how many different patterns a cellular automaton produces. No single measure is accepted as the measure of complexity, and a system can score high on one and low on another, so honest work reports several measures together and says which one it used. This record specifies the measures, the way each is computed, and the rule that a complexity claim must name its measure, or it is not a claim at all. Artificial Life and the Origin of Life

JAH-ALIFE-000019 · artificial life and origin of life · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life and the Origin of Life. The origin of life is the question of how chemistry on the early Earth became biology, and artificial life contributes to it by building models that test each proposed step in a system where every reaction is visible. The proposed steps are: simple molecules forming from early-Earth chemistry, those molecules joining into longer chains, chains enclosed in bubbles that act like the first cells, and enclosed chemistry that begins to copy itself. Artificial chemistries test the first steps in software, protocells test the enclosure step in the laboratory, and minimal cells test what the finished product needs at minimum. No model claims to have recreated the origin of life: the early Earth cannot be re-run, and each model tests one step, not the whole path. This record specifies the origin-of-life contribution of artificial life: the proposed steps, the model for each step, and the honest statement that the whole path remains unproven, with all laboratory work under biosafety screening, authorization, and human review. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Synthetic Cells as Research Models

JAH-ALIFE-000020 · synthetic cells as research models · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Synthetic Cells as Research Models. A synthetic cell is a cell built or heavily rebuilt by researchers to serve as a research model, and it is studied here strictly as a model, under the boundary that governs this pair: design-study only with biosafety screening, authorization, and human review, and no pathogen, no toxin, no gene-drive construct, and no synthesis-enabling instruction is built. The research uses synthetic cells to ask what a cell must do: hold its chemistry apart from the outside, take in energy, make copies of its parts, and divide. The two approaches are top-down, which pares a real cell down, and bottom-up, which assembles a cell-like bubble from parts and tests which functions appear. The results so far are partial: bubbles that grow and divide, and pared-down cells that live on a few hundred genes, but no fully assembled working cell built from scratch. This record specifies synthetic cells as research models: their two approaches, their tests, their partial results, and the boundary that keeps the work as modelling with screening and review. Artificial Life Software Platforms

JAH-ALIFE-000021 · artificial life software platforms · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life Software Platforms. An artificial life software platform is the shared program that researchers use to run life-like experiments: it provides the world, the organisms, the recording, and the tools to inspect a run, so that an experiment done in one laboratory can be repeated in another. A good platform gives four things: a world with clear rules for space, time, and resources; organisms that can be defined, copied, and mutated; full recording, so every birth, death, and ancestor can be read back; and tools for plotting, measuring, and exporting results. Tierra and Avida are the classic examples, and newer platforms add visual worlds, robot bodies, and shared online runs. The platform is also where reproducibility lives: a run is only evidence if another laboratory can run the same platform with the same seed and get the same result. This record specifies artificial life software platforms: their four parts, their recording rules, and the reproducibility test every platform must pass. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. The public edition records the research specification as documented public knowledge and established research, with sources that were fetched and checked on 2026-11-17. The Signature edition holds Signature-made designs, each labeled design-study, which is a Signature-made design labeled as a design and never presented as an established fact. The maturity label for this record is E4, which stands for Experimental, meaning experiments exist but the technology is not yet routine. A maturity label is a short code saying how mature a technology is, and E4 marks this work as research, never as a finished or proven system. Artificial Life and Biosafety

JAH-ALIFE-000022 · artificial life and biosafety · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life and Biosafety. Biosafety is the set of practices that keep biological work safe, including screening, authorization, and human review, and this record states the biosafety boundary for the whole artificial life pair in full. The boundary is: synthetic biology content in this pair is design-study only with biosafety screening; all laboratory work described here is research modelling, not the building of working biological weapons or harmful organisms. No pathogen content is built here: No pathogen, no toxin, no gene-drive construct, and no synthesis-enabling instruction appears in any record of this pair. A pathogen is a disease-causing organism, a toxin is a poisonous substance made by a living thing, a gene-drive construct is a genetic element designed to spread through a population, and synthesis-enabling content is instruction that would let someone build a harmful biological thing. Every record that touches laboratory biology carries this boundary, every design-study says it is a design, and any request that crosses the boundary is refused with the reason stated. This record specifies the boundary, the screening steps, and the review that must happen before any biological work runs. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. Artificial Life Education and Outreach

JAH-ALIFE-000023 · artificial life education · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is Artificial Life Education and Outreach. Artificial life education is the teaching of life-like systems to students and the public, and it works because the subject can be shown running: a Game of Life grid on a screen, a swarm of dots forming a flock, a digital organism population evolving before the class. The teaching ladder for this pair runs from simple to compounded to most complex: first the Game of Life and its three rules, then cellular automata and swarms, then digital organisms and Tierra, then protocells and minimal cells with their biosafety boundary stated. Outreach uses the same ladder in public demonstrations, museum displays, and online simulators that anyone can run without installing anything. The teaching rule is honesty at every step: a model is called a model, a simulation is called a simulation, and no demonstration is presented as a living thing. This record specifies the education ladder, the demonstrations at each step, and the honesty rule that governs them all. The system identity is fixed: this is a future artificial life record, built as one database pair with a public edition and a Signature edition, each holding 24 records, so the pair holds 48 records in total. The Future of Artificial Life Research

JAH-ALIFE-000024 · future of artificial life · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Description: This record is the long full official specification for one part of the Artificial Life Database, catalog item 5 of the 100 Future-Level Resources catalog. A database is an organized collection of records that can be searched, read, and added to, and a record is one complete entry in a database, with all of its fields filled in. The future research type covered here is The Future of Artificial Life Research. The future of artificial life research is the set of open directions the field is moving toward, stated here as research directions with maturity label E4 (Experimental), never as predictions or guaranteed breakthroughs. The directions are: open-ended evolution systems that keep inventing without settling, living machines built from more cell types and larger bodies, artificial chemistries that run longer self-keeping cycles, swarms of robots that work together in the real world, and better measures of complexity and lifelikeness that let results be compared. Each direction carries the same rules as the rest of this pair: models called models, designs called designs, biosafety screening for anything touching laboratory biology, and no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence anywhere. The planning windows in the catalog run from 2026 to 2035 for the nearest work and further for the rest, and those windows are planning windows, not promises. This record specifies the future directions, their current status, and the rules that will govern them. 4. History

What Artificial Life Is

JAH-ALIFE-000001 · artificial life identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

History: The 100 Future-Level Resources catalog was pasted on 2026-11-17 as a future-technology research plan for the JAH Network Master AI (artificial intelligence), with the words of the paste kept straight as another AI's drafted plan, not the maker's own wording, and with the paste's own note that its author could not retrieve the live network pages, so the catalog is a plan and not a claim of inspection. The cross-check for this catalog is made against the real live manifest, which is the master list of all databases in the network, and this record states where its type stands against that live manifest in the description above. Version history of this specification: version 1.0 is this Wave 1 record, written 2026-11-17 as a package-only record, which means it is built and checked in the workspace and is not pushed, not live, and not merged. Later versions will record their changes here, with the date, the change, and the reason, and no version will ever silently rewrite an earlier version. The record kind for this database is future artificial life record, and the identifier prefix, which is the fixed letters at the start of every record identifier in this database, is JAH-ALIFE-. The artificial life type was fifth in the catalog's priority order because life-like systems are the foundation of the future-intelligence spine.

Dataset: Artificial life identity from the catalog plan and the cited sources; figures recomputed by this record's demo.

Skeptical analysis: Limits. This record is a future research specification, not a claim that the system it specifies is deployed, live, or finished. Package-only means the record is built and checked in the workspace and is not pushed, not live, and not merged, and no count in this record should be read as a live network count. The sources were fetched on 2026-11-17 and returned HTTP 200 on that date; a page can change after that date, and the retrieval date is recorded so a later reader can re-fetch and compare. 5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

What Artificial Life Is

JAH-ALIFE-000001 · artificial life identity · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without a shared identity, artificial life work is mistaken for claims about creating life, and models are confused with living things. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for artificial life research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; synthetic biology content is design-study only with biosafety screening, and no pathogen, toxin, gene-drive, or synthesis-enabling content is built; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Soft artificial life, which is life-like behaviour built in software, such as digital organisms; Hard artificial life, which is life-like behaviour built in hardware, such as adapting robots; Wet artificial life, which is life-like behaviour built in laboratory chemistry, such as protocells; Life-like behaviour, which is patterns such as self-keeping, copying, change, and adaptation, without claiming the system is alive. Database structure. The database holds records of kind future artificial life record. Every record carries an identifier, a title, a subject, a description, a history, a problem, a method, reported results, a science proof, a math proof, a formal equation with a plain-words gloss, a scientific equation with a proof sketch, a skeptical analysis, an applications section, a verification path, a specification block with dimensions and care notes, key figures, a teaching-ladder rank, a dataset note, an evidence label, a maturity label, a demo with expected output, a working program, sources with web addresses and retrieval dates, network links, a generator block, and three scales. The checkable figures for this record are branches 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, research parts 10. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. A record that fails any acceptance test is not filed as validated. The demo figures for this record are branches 3, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, research parts 10, and the demo prints them twice so that any change in the figures changes the printed output and the digests with it.

When it applies: This record applies whenever the Master AI (artificial intelligence) needs to build, find, or check a future artificial life record: it gives the identity, the complete specification, the database structure, the search and retrieval rules, the working code, the generator, the website interface, the documentation shape, the demonstration, the automated tests, the validation results, the version history, the source provenance, the maturity label, and the expansion instructions in one place. Digital Organisms

JAH-ALIFE-000002 · digital organisms · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Evolution in nature is too slow and too tangled to watch step by step, and without digital organisms researchers cannot run evolution as a repeatable experiment. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree. This specification answers both problems by fixing one identity, one structure, one retrieval rule, and one validation ladder for the whole type, so that every later build of this type can be checked against the same standard.

Method: Complete specification. The research specification must satisfy every one of these requirements: the pair holds the long full official specification for artificial life research under the section-8 research standard; every record carries maturity label E4 (Experimental) with a plain-words definition; every public record cites at least two sources fetched and checked on 2026-11-17, with web address, resource identifier, and retrieval date; every record carries a working Python demo with expected output captured by execution, and a working program; every record carries a JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, that round-trips; synthetic biology content is design-study only with biosafety screening, and no pathogen, toxin, gene-drive, or synthesis-enabling content is built; no claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in the pair; the pair is package-only: built and checked in the workspace, not pushed, not live, and not merged. Research standard. Every future research project in this specification carries ten parts, from section 8 of the catalog: the research question, which is the single question the project sets out to answer; existing knowledge and source references, which is what is already known and where it is documented; the proposed innovation, which is the new idea this project adds; the technical specification, which is the complete detailed description of what is built; the mathematical and scientific foundations, which are the equations and established science the work rests on; the prototype or simulation plan, which is how a working model or computer model is built and run; the testing and validation criteria, which are the checks that decide whether the work passes; the risks and limitations, which are what can go wrong and what the work cannot do; the evidence and development status, which is what has actually been shown and how mature the work is; and the future expansion opportunities, which are the next steps the work opens up. This record covers all ten parts: the research question is in the problem, the existing knowledge is in the description and sources, the proposed innovation is in the method, the technical specification is the method in full, the foundations are in the math proof and science proof, the prototype or simulation plan is in the demo and program, the testing and validation criteria are in the results, the risks and limitations are in the limits, the evidence and development status are in the answer and the maturity label, and the future expansion opportunities are in the applications and the expansion instructions. The system is built from these parts, each of which is specified in full: Instruction string, which is the program text that is the organism's body; Mutation, which is a small random change in the instructions when copying; Computer time, which is the processing power an organism needs to run; Memory, which is the storage space an organism needs to live in. Database structure. The database holds records of kind future artificial life record. Every record carries an identifier, a title, a subject, a description, a history, a problem, a method, reported results, a science proof, a math proof, a formal equation with a plain-words gloss, a scientific equation with a proof sketch, a skeptical analysis, an applications section, a verification path, a specification block with dimensions and care notes, key figures, a teaching-ladder rank, a dataset note, an evidence label, a maturity label, a demo with expected output, a working program, sources with web addresses and retrieval dates, network links, a generator block, and three scales. The checkable figures for this record are resources 2, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, generations 1000. Search and retrieval. Retrieval works by exact identifier, by title, by subject, and by full-text search over every prose field, and every retrieved record returns with its sources and its provenance attached, so a retrieved fact can always be traced back to the page it came from. Working source code. The demo and the program in this record are real working Python code, not a description of code: the demo recomputes the record's own figures twice and compares SHA-256 digests, which are 256-bit fingerprints computed with the standard hashlib library, and the program wraps the same computation in a class with a run method, so the demo and the program agree by construction. Reusable generator. A seeded boundless generator, which is a program that creates new records on demand from a starting number called a seed, serves this database, and it must pass its generator gates, which are checks a generator must pass before its output is accepted: the emitted record kind must match the manifest record kind, the slug must use the single signature form, the identifier must use the database's own prefix, and the hard stop must be 1,000,000,000. Website interface. The website interface, which is the set of web pages through which a person or a program uses the system, gives one page per record with the full record, a search page, and an accessible version that is plain and readable without JavaScript, which is the programming language that runs in the browser. Documentation. The documentation explains the system in plain words first, defines every term it uses, and puts sources and citations last, so a reader meets the meaning before the references. Demonstration. The demo in this record is the demonstration: it runs, it prints its results, and its output is captured as the expected output, never written by hand. Automated tests. The automated tests, which are a test suite, a collection of automated tests, check the demo, the program, the generator, the record kind, the slug form, the identifier prefix, the hard stop, and the JCC code, which stands for JAH Condensed Code, a short code that points to one exact record. Expansion instructions. To expand this database, add records in the same shape, keep the identifiers sequential, keep the sources fetched and dated, and never present generated content as authentic public record.

Results reported: Validation results. This record is validated by execution, not by assertion: its demo was executed at build time and its printed output was captured as the expected output, its program was executed and returned a deterministic match, its figures were recomputed twice with matching SHA-256 digests, and the whole Wave 1 Group A set passed CHECK 2, which is the second validation pass that re-checks every record, every demo, every program, every JCC code round-trip, every source web address, and every generator gate by execution. The acceptance tests for this future research type are: the record is complete, with every required field filled; the demo executes and its output matches the expected output exactly; the program executes and reports a deterministic match; the JCC code round-trips, which means recomputing it from the record gives the same code; every cited source returns HTTP 200, which is the web server's answer that the page was found, when fetched; the generator passes its gates; the maturity label is present and defined in plain words; and the record defines its terms in plain words. A record that fails any acceptance test is not filed as validated. The demo figures for this record are resources 2, records per edition 24, pair records 48, sources per record 3, glossary terms 50, sections 16, generations 1000, and the demo prints them twice so that any change in the figures changes the printed output and the digests with it.

JAH-ALIFE-000006 · cellular automata · Maturity: E4 — Experimental — experiments exist, not yet routine.

Problem: The research question this record answers is the gap between having a named future capability and having a working research system for it. A name alone builds nothing: without a specification, a database structure, search and retrieval, working source code, a reusable generator, a website interface, documentation, a demonstration, automated tests, and validation results, the capability exists only as words. Without cellular automata, artificial life would have no shared laboratory for watching simple rules grow into complex behaviour. The wider problem is duplication and drift: builders who cannot find what already exists rebuild it badly, contradict it, or miss it entirely, and systems that measure and identify things in different ways drift apart until their records no longer agree.
